========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because there’s usually this point where part of me is very aware of what’s happening, like I’m standing there in my kitchen or sitting on the couch and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, put the rest away, you’ve had enough. But then I don’t actually stop.

It almost feels like I’m watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. I’ll open the takeout leftovers, then something from the cupboard, then something else, and it keeps going until there’s not really anything left that I had been eating. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. It’s more like I can’t get myself to pause long enough to choose differently.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… not great. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. Sometimes disgusted with myself, but even saying that feels kind of dramatic. It’s more like this heavy, sinking feeling, like, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll start thinking about how much I ate and replaying it, like the order of everything, what I opened, what I finished, what I should’ve just thrown away or not bought in the first place.

I usually feel really ashamed, especially if there are wrappers or containers out where I can see them. I don’t want anyone to know, even though I live alone, so it’s not like someone is standing there judging me. But I’m judging myself pretty hard. I’ll think I’m an adult, I’m 40, I should be able to just eat dinner like a normal person and be done. And then I get mad at myself for not having that basic control.

It kind of hangs over me afterward. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but it’s mostly embarrassment and shame, and feeling like I let myself down again.

It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because there’s usually this point where part of me is very aware of what’s happening, like I’m standing there in my kitchen or sitting on the couch and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, put the rest away, you’ve had enough. But then I don’t actually stop.\n\nIt almost feels like I’m watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. I’ll open the takeout leftovers, then something from the cupboard, then something else, and it keeps going until there’s not really anything left that I had been eating. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. It’s more like I can’t get myself to pause long enough to choose differently.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating is over?\n\nPatient: I mean… not great. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. Sometimes disgusted with myself, but even saying that feels kind of dramatic. It’s more like this heavy, sinking feeling, like, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll start thinking about how much I ate and replaying it, like the order of everything, what I opened, what I finished, what I should’ve just thrown away or not bought in the first place.\n\nI usually feel really ashamed, especially if there are wrappers or containers out where I can see them. I don’t want anyone to know, even though I live alone, so it’s not like someone is standing there judging me. But I’m judging myself pretty hard. I’ll think I’m an adult, I’m 40, I should be able to just eat dinner like a normal person and be done. And then I get mad at myself for not having that basic control.\n\nIt kind of hangs over me afterward. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but it’s mostly embarrassment and shame, and feeling like I let myself down again.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / entertainment_attendant_or_related_worker / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, guilt is probably part of it, but I’m not totally sure where guilt ends and shame starts, if that makes sense. I do have this feeling afterward like I did something wrong, even though it’s just food and I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud.

It’s more like I’ll sit there thinking, “Why did I do that again?” and I’ll feel bad about wasting money, or eating things I had meant to save, or just not stopping when I told myself to stop. So yes, there’s guilt in that way. But the bigger feeling is usually embarrassment and being really disappointed in myself. Like I’ve broken some promise to myself that I didn’t even say out loud.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Lately it’s been… most weeks, I’d say. Since my apartment move kind of settled down and my routine became more predictable, it’s usually at least once a week that I’ll have one of those late-night episodes.

It tends to happen at night, after work or after I’ve been home for a while, when I’m by myself. I’ll start out thinking I’m just having something small or finishing dinner, and then it turns into eating way past what I meant to. Not every single night or anything like that, but most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.

And then afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow, like throwing up, or telling myself I’m just going to skip breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a really long run even if I don’t actually feel up to it. So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.

In the moment it sort of turns into this long stretch of eating, and then afterward I look at the containers or the bags or whatever and I’m kind of shocked and embarrassed by how much I actually ate. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because there’s usually this point where part of me is very aware of what’s happening, like I’m standing there in my kitchen or sitting on the couch and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, put the rest away, you’ve had enough. But then I don’t actually stop.\n\nIt almost feels like I’m watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. I’ll open the takeout leftovers, then something from the cupboard, then something else, and it keeps going until there’s not really anything left that I had been eating. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. It’s more like I can’t get myself to pause long enough to choose differently.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating is over?\n\nPatient: I mean… not great. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. Sometimes disgusted with myself, but even saying that feels kind of dramatic. It’s more like this heavy, sinking feeling, like, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll start thinking about how much I ate and replaying it, like the order of everything, what I opened, what I finished, what I should’ve just thrown away or not bought in the first place.\n\nI usually feel really ashamed, especially if there are wrappers or containers out where I can see them. I don’t want anyone to know, even though I live alone, so it’s not like someone is standing there judging me. But I’m judging myself pretty hard. I’ll think I’m an adult, I’m 40, I should be able to just eat dinner like a normal person and be done. And then I get mad at myself for not having that basic control.\n\nIt kind of hangs over me afterward. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but it’s mostly embarrassment and shame, and feeling like I let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilt is probably part of it, but I’m not totally sure where guilt ends and shame starts, if that makes sense. I do have this feeling afterward like I did something wrong, even though it’s just food and I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll sit there thinking, “Why did I do that again?” and I’ll feel bad about wasting money, or eating things I had meant to save, or just not stopping when I told myself to stop. So yes, there’s guilt in that way. But the bigger feeling is usually embarrassment and being really disappointed in myself. Like I’ve broken some promise to myself that I didn’t even say out loud.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… most weeks, I’d say. Since my apartment move kind of settled down and my routine became more predictable, it’s usually at least once a week that I’ll have one of those late-night episodes.\n\nIt tends to happen at night, after work or after I’ve been home for a while, when I’m by myself. I’ll start out thinking I’m just having something small or finishing dinner, and then it turns into eating way past what I meant to. Not every single night or anything like that, but most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.\n\nAnd then afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow, like throwing up, or telling myself I’m just going to skip breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a really long run even if I don’t actually feel up to it. So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.

A lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.

Other times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.

It’s not always some big planned thing. It’s more this pressure in my head, like I owe a debt from the night before and I need to pay it back somehow.

So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to make up for these episodes, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.\n\nOther times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.

It’s not every single time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks, I’ll end up going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. Usually it’s after I already feel like I went way past the point where I should’ve stopped, and then I start panicking about it just… sitting in me, I guess. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.

I know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. Then afterward I don’t exactly feel good, it’s more like relief mixed with feeling pretty ashamed that I did it again.

It’s… not every time. I want to be clear about that, because sometimes I’ll just sit with the awful feeling afterward, or I’ll tell myself I won’t eat the next morning, but there are times where I do make myself throw up.

I’d say it happens sometimes after those bigger episodes, especially when I’ve gotten home and eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks and then I start feeling panicky, like it’s just sitting in me. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. It feels like, if I don’t get it out, it’s going to turn into weight, even though I know that probably sounds… not logical.

So, frequency-wise, I don’t know the exact number. It’s not daily, and it’s not after every episode, but it does happen sometimes when I feel like I’ve really gone overboard.

So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to make up for these episodes, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.\n\nOther times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing. It’s more this pressure in my head, like I owe a debt from the night before and I need to pay it back somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks, I’ll end up going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. Usually it’s after I already feel like I went way past the point where I should’ve stopped, and then I start panicking about it just… sitting in me, I guess. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. I’m honestly not totally sure. I mean, I notice my weight, probably more than I should, but I don’t know that I can say there’s been some huge, clear change over time because of it.

It kind of feels like it goes up and down a little, and I’ll get really focused on those small changes, like if my clothes feel tighter one day or the number is different, I’ll read way too much into it. But I don’t think I’ve had some dramatic weight loss or gain that I could point to and say, “That’s from the eating episodes and then trying to make up for them.”

It’s more that I’m constantly worried it’s going to change, if that makes sense. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. I’ll open the takeout leftovers, then something from the cupboard, then something else, and it keeps going until there’s not really anything left that I had been eating. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. It’s more like I can’t get myself to pause long enough to choose differently.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating is over?\n\nPatient: I mean… not great. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. Sometimes disgusted with myself, but even saying that feels kind of dramatic. It’s more like this heavy, sinking feeling, like, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll start thinking about how much I ate and replaying it, like the order of everything, what I opened, what I finished, what I should’ve just thrown away or not bought in the first place.\n\nI usually feel really ashamed, especially if there are wrappers or containers out where I can see them. I don’t want anyone to know, even though I live alone, so it’s not like someone is standing there judging me. But I’m judging myself pretty hard. I’ll think I’m an adult, I’m 40, I should be able to just eat dinner like a normal person and be done. And then I get mad at myself for not having that basic control.\n\nIt kind of hangs over me afterward. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but it’s mostly embarrassment and shame, and feeling like I let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilt is probably part of it, but I’m not totally sure where guilt ends and shame starts, if that makes sense. I do have this feeling afterward like I did something wrong, even though it’s just food and I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll sit there thinking, “Why did I do that again?” and I’ll feel bad about wasting money, or eating things I had meant to save, or just not stopping when I told myself to stop. So yes, there’s guilt in that way. But the bigger feeling is usually embarrassment and being really disappointed in myself. Like I’ve broken some promise to myself that I didn’t even say out loud.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… most weeks, I’d say. Since my apartment move kind of settled down and my routine became more predictable, it’s usually at least once a week that I’ll have one of those late-night episodes.\n\nIt tends to happen at night, after work or after I’ve been home for a while, when I’m by myself. I’ll start out thinking I’m just having something small or finishing dinner, and then it turns into eating way past what I meant to. Not every single night or anything like that, but most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.\n\nAnd then afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow, like throwing up, or telling myself I’m just going to skip breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a really long run even if I don’t actually feel up to it. So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to make up for these episodes, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.\n\nOther times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing. It’s more this pressure in my head, like I owe a debt from the night before and I need to pay it back somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks, I’ll end up going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. Usually it’s after I already feel like I went way past the point where I should’ve stopped, and then I start panicking about it just… sitting in me, I guess. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. Then afterward I don’t exactly feel good, it’s more like relief mixed with feeling pretty ashamed that I did it again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every time. I want to be clear about that, because sometimes I’ll just sit with the awful feeling afterward, or I’ll tell myself I won’t eat the next morning, but there are times where I do make myself throw up.\n\nI’d say it happens sometimes after those bigger episodes, especially when I’ve gotten home and eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks and then I start feeling panicky, like it’s just sitting in me. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. It feels like, if I don’t get it out, it’s going to turn into weight, even though I know that probably sounds… not logical.\n\nSo, frequency-wise, I don’t know the exact number. It’s not daily, and it’s not after every episode, but it does happen sometimes when I feel like I’ve really gone overboard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes and your attempts to make up for them?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I mean, I notice my weight, probably more than I should, but I don’t know that I can say there’s been some huge, clear change over time because of it.\n\nIt kind of feels like it goes up and down a little, and I’ll get really focused on those small changes, like if my clothes feel tighter one day or the number is different, I’ll read way too much into it. But I don’t think I’ve had some dramatic weight loss or gain that I could point to and say, “That’s from the eating episodes and then trying to make up for them.”\n\nIt’s more that I’m constantly worried it’s going to change, if that makes sense. Like the fear of gaining weight is there, and that drives a lot of what I do afterward, but as far as actual significant changes… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything major, or at least nothing I’m confident saying is significant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

=====================================

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow when I say it out loud, but honestly, yes. It’s the fear of gaining weight, or even just looking like I gained weight.

Like, I work around people all day at the venue, and I’m visible. Guests see me, coworkers see me, and even when people have told me I look too lean or that I don’t need to worry, I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel even a little tighter, or if the scale is up by like a pound, I get this immediate panic feeling. It’s like my brain jumps straight to, “Everyone’s going to notice. They’re going to think I let myself go.”

And I know one pound probably isn’t something anyone else can see. I can say that logically. But in the moment it feels very real, like I have to do something to undo it before it shows. So yeah, that fear is what pushes me to try to make up for it, whether that’s not eating as much afterward or purging. It’s not really about feeling healthy or anything like that. It’s more like I’m trying to stop that panic and stop myself from feeling like I’m suddenly different or bigger.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.5, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I came in because I’ve been having these times, usually after I work a late shift, where I’ll stop and get food on the way home, like a full takeout meal, and then once I’m back at my apartment I just… keep going. Chips, sweets, leftovers, whatever is around, really.\n\nAnd it’s not like I planned to eat all of it. In the moment it sort of turns into this long stretch of eating, and then afterward I look at the containers or the bags or whatever and I’m kind of shocked and embarrassed by how much I actually ate. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because there’s usually this point where part of me is very aware of what’s happening, like I’m standing there in my kitchen or sitting on the couch and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, put the rest away, you’ve had enough. But then I don’t actually stop.\n\nIt almost feels like I’m watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. I’ll open the takeout leftovers, then something from the cupboard, then something else, and it keeps going until there’s not really anything left that I had been eating. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. It’s more like I can’t get myself to pause long enough to choose differently.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating is over?\n\nPatient: I mean… not great. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. Sometimes disgusted with myself, but even saying that feels kind of dramatic. It’s more like this heavy, sinking feeling, like, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll start thinking about how much I ate and replaying it, like the order of everything, what I opened, what I finished, what I should’ve just thrown away or not bought in the first place.\n\nI usually feel really ashamed, especially if there are wrappers or containers out where I can see them. I don’t want anyone to know, even though I live alone, so it’s not like someone is standing there judging me. But I’m judging myself pretty hard. I’ll think I’m an adult, I’m 40, I should be able to just eat dinner like a normal person and be done. And then I get mad at myself for not having that basic control.\n\nIt kind of hangs over me afterward. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but it’s mostly embarrassment and shame, and feeling like I let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilt is probably part of it, but I’m not totally sure where guilt ends and shame starts, if that makes sense. I do have this feeling afterward like I did something wrong, even though it’s just food and I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll sit there thinking, “Why did I do that again?” and I’ll feel bad about wasting money, or eating things I had meant to save, or just not stopping when I told myself to stop. So yes, there’s guilt in that way. But the bigger feeling is usually embarrassment and being really disappointed in myself. Like I’ve broken some promise to myself that I didn’t even say out loud.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… most weeks, I’d say. Since my apartment move kind of settled down and my routine became more predictable, it’s usually at least once a week that I’ll have one of those late-night episodes.\n\nIt tends to happen at night, after work or after I’ve been home for a while, when I’m by myself. I’ll start out thinking I’m just having something small or finishing dinner, and then it turns into eating way past what I meant to. Not every single night or anything like that, but most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.\n\nAnd then afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow, like throwing up, or telling myself I’m just going to skip breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a really long run even if I don’t actually feel up to it. So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to make up for these episodes, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.\n\nOther times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing. It’s more this pressure in my head, like I owe a debt from the night before and I need to pay it back somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks, I’ll end up going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. Usually it’s after I already feel like I went way past the point where I should’ve stopped, and then I start panicking about it just… sitting in me, I guess. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. Then afterward I don’t exactly feel good, it’s more like relief mixed with feeling pretty ashamed that I did it again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every time. I want to be clear about that, because sometimes I’ll just sit with the awful feeling afterward, or I’ll tell myself I won’t eat the next morning, but there are times where I do make myself throw up.\n\nI’d say it happens sometimes after those bigger episodes, especially when I’ve gotten home and eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks and then I start feeling panicky, like it’s just sitting in me. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. It feels like, if I don’t get it out, it’s going to turn into weight, even though I know that probably sounds… not logical.\n\nSo, frequency-wise, I don’t know the exact number. It’s not daily, and it’s not after every episode, but it does happen sometimes when I feel like I’ve really gone overboard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes and your attempts to make up for them?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I mean, I notice my weight, probably more than I should, but I don’t know that I can say there’s been some huge, clear change over time because of it.\n\nIt kind of feels like it goes up and down a little, and I’ll get really focused on those small changes, like if my clothes feel tighter one day or the number is different, I’ll read way too much into it. But I don’t think I’ve had some dramatic weight loss or gain that I could point to and say, “That’s from the eating episodes and then trying to make up for them.”\n\nIt’s more that I’m constantly worried it’s going to change, if that makes sense. Like the fear of gaining weight is there, and that drives a lot of what I do afterward, but as far as actual significant changes… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything major, or at least nothing I’m confident saying is significant.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight what motivates you to try to make up for these episodes of eating by restricting your food intake or purging?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow when I say it out loud, but honestly, yes. It’s the fear of gaining weight, or even just looking like I gained weight.\n\nLike, I work around people all day at the venue, and I’m visible. Guests see me, coworkers see me, and even when people have told me I look too lean or that I don’t need to worry, I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel even a little tighter, or if the scale is up by like a pound, I get this immediate panic feeling. It’s like my brain jumps straight to, “Everyone’s going to notice. They’re going to think I let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know one pound probably isn’t something anyone else can see. I can say that logically. But in the moment it feels very real, like I have to do something to undo it before it shows. So yeah, that fear is what pushes me to try to make up for it, whether that’s not eating as much afterward or purging. It’s not really about feeling healthy or anything like that. It’s more like I’m trying to stop that panic and stop myself from feeling like I’m suddenly different or bigger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every time. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. It feels like, if I don’t get it out, it’s going to turn into weight, even though I know that probably sounds… not logical.\n\nSo, frequency-wise, I don’t know the exact number. It’s not daily, and it’s not after every episode, but it does happen sometimes when I feel like I’ve really gone overboard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes and your attempts to make up for them?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I mean, I notice my weight, probably more than I should, but I don’t know that I can say there’s been some huge, clear change over time because of it.\n\nIt kind of feels like it goes up and down a little, and I’ll get really focused on those small changes, like if my clothes feel tighter one day or the number is different, I’ll read way too much into it. But I don’t think I’ve had some dramatic weight loss or gain that I could point to and say, “That’s from the eating episodes and then trying to make up for them.”\n\nIt’s more that I’m constantly worried it’s going to change, if that makes sense. Like the fear of gaining weight is there, and that drives a lot of what I do afterward, but as far as actual significant changes… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything major, or at least nothing I’m confident saying is significant.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight what motivates you to try to make up for these episodes of eating by restricting your food intake or purging?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow when I say it out loud, but honestly, yes. It’s the fear of gaining weight, or even just looking like I gained weight.\n\nLike, I work around people all day at the venue, and I’m visible. Guests see me, coworkers see me, and even when people have told me I look too lean or that I don’t need to worry, I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel even a little tighter, or if the scale is up by like a pound, I get this immediate panic feeling. It’s like my brain jumps straight to, “Everyone’s going to notice. They’re going to think I let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know one pound probably isn’t something anyone else can see. I can say that logically. But in the moment it feels very real, like I have to do something to undo it before it shows. So yeah, that fear is what pushes me to try to make up for it, whether that’s not eating as much afterward or purging. It’s not really about feeling healthy or anything like that. It’s more like I’m trying to stop that panic and stop myself from feeling like I’m suddenly different or bigger.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow many times a month would you say you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say… maybe a few times a month? It’s hard to give an exact number because I don’t really track it, and honestly I try not to think of it that way. But if I’m estimating, it’s probably around two or three times in a month, maybe a little more in a bad month.

It’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, like I’ll keep picking at things and then suddenly it feels like I crossed some line. Then I get this panicky feeling, like it’s just sitting in me and it’s going to turn into weight if I don’t do something. That’s when I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.

So… not every week like clockwork, and not after every time I overeat, but sometimes. A few times a month is probably the closest answer.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I came in because I’ve been having these times, usually after I work a late shift, where I’ll stop and get food on the way home, like a full takeout meal, and then once I’m back at my apartment I just… keep going. Chips, sweets, leftovers, whatever is around, really.\n\nAnd it’s not like I planned to eat all of it. In the moment it sort of turns into this long stretch of eating, and then afterward I look at the containers or the bags or whatever and I’m kind of shocked and embarrassed by how much I actually ate. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because there’s usually this point where part of me is very aware of what’s happening, like I’m standing there in my kitchen or sitting on the couch and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, put the rest away, you’ve had enough. But then I don’t actually stop.\n\nIt almost feels like I’m watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. I’ll open the takeout leftovers, then something from the cupboard, then something else, and it keeps going until there’s not really anything left that I had been eating. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. It’s more like I can’t get myself to pause long enough to choose differently.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating is over?\n\nPatient: I mean… not great. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. Sometimes disgusted with myself, but even saying that feels kind of dramatic. It’s more like this heavy, sinking feeling, like, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll start thinking about how much I ate and replaying it, like the order of everything, what I opened, what I finished, what I should’ve just thrown away or not bought in the first place.\n\nI usually feel really ashamed, especially if there are wrappers or containers out where I can see them. I don’t want anyone to know, even though I live alone, so it’s not like someone is standing there judging me. But I’m judging myself pretty hard. I’ll think I’m an adult, I’m 40, I should be able to just eat dinner like a normal person and be done. And then I get mad at myself for not having that basic control.\n\nIt kind of hangs over me afterward. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but it’s mostly embarrassment and shame, and feeling like I let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilt is probably part of it, but I’m not totally sure where guilt ends and shame starts, if that makes sense. I do have this feeling afterward like I did something wrong, even though it’s just food and I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll sit there thinking, “Why did I do that again?” and I’ll feel bad about wasting money, or eating things I had meant to save, or just not stopping when I told myself to stop. So yes, there’s guilt in that way. But the bigger feeling is usually embarrassment and being really disappointed in myself. Like I’ve broken some promise to myself that I didn’t even say out loud.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… most weeks, I’d say. Since my apartment move kind of settled down and my routine became more predictable, it’s usually at least once a week that I’ll have one of those late-night episodes.\n\nIt tends to happen at night, after work or after I’ve been home for a while, when I’m by myself. I’ll start out thinking I’m just having something small or finishing dinner, and then it turns into eating way past what I meant to. Not every single night or anything like that, but most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.\n\nAnd then afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow, like throwing up, or telling myself I’m just going to skip breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a really long run even if I don’t actually feel up to it. So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to make up for these episodes, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.\n\nOther times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing. It’s more this pressure in my head, like I owe a debt from the night before and I need to pay it back somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks, I’ll end up going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. Usually it’s after I already feel like I went way past the point where I should’ve stopped, and then I start panicking about it just… sitting in me, I guess. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. Then afterward I don’t exactly feel good, it’s more like relief mixed with feeling pretty ashamed that I did it again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every time. I want to be clear about that, because sometimes I’ll just sit with the awful feeling afterward, or I’ll tell myself I won’t eat the next morning, but there are times where I do make myself throw up.\n\nI’d say it happens sometimes after those bigger episodes, especially when I’ve gotten home and eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks and then I start feeling panicky, like it’s just sitting in me. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. It feels like, if I don’t get it out, it’s going to turn into weight, even though I know that probably sounds… not logical.\n\nSo, frequency-wise, I don’t know the exact number. It’s not daily, and it’s not after every episode, but it does happen sometimes when I feel like I’ve really gone overboard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes and your attempts to make up for them?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. Then afterward I don’t exactly feel good, it’s more like relief mixed with feeling pretty ashamed that I did it again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every time. I want to be clear about that, because sometimes I’ll just sit with the awful feeling afterward, or I’ll tell myself I won’t eat the next morning, but there are times where I do make myself throw up.\n\nI’d say it happens sometimes after those bigger episodes, especially when I’ve gotten home and eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks and then I start feeling panicky, like it’s just sitting in me. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. It feels like, if I don’t get it out, it’s going to turn into weight, even though I know that probably sounds… not logical.\n\nSo, frequency-wise, I don’t know the exact number. It’s not daily, and it’s not after every episode, but it does happen sometimes when I feel like I’ve really gone overboard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes and your attempts to make up for them?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I mean, I notice my weight, probably more than I should, but I don’t know that I can say there’s been some huge, clear change over time because of it.\n\nIt kind of feels like it goes up and down a little, and I’ll get really focused on those small changes, like if my clothes feel tighter one day or the number is different, I’ll read way too much into it. But I don’t think I’ve had some dramatic weight loss or gain that I could point to and say, “That’s from the eating episodes and then trying to make up for them.”\n\nIt’s more that I’m constantly worried it’s going to change, if that makes sense. Like the fear of gaining weight is there, and that drives a lot of what I do afterward, but as far as actual significant changes… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything major, or at least nothing I’m confident saying is significant.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight what motivates you to try to make up for these episodes of eating by restricting your food intake or purging?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow when I say it out loud, but honestly, yes. It’s the fear of gaining weight, or even just looking like I gained weight.\n\nLike, I work around people all day at the venue, and I’m visible. Guests see me, coworkers see me, and even when people have told me I look too lean or that I don’t need to worry, I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel even a little tighter, or if the scale is up by like a pound, I get this immediate panic feeling. It’s like my brain jumps straight to, “Everyone’s going to notice. They’re going to think I let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know one pound probably isn’t something anyone else can see. I can say that logically. But in the moment it feels very real, like I have to do something to undo it before it shows. So yeah, that fear is what pushes me to try to make up for it, whether that’s not eating as much afterward or purging. It’s not really about feeling healthy or anything like that. It’s more like I’m trying to stop that panic and stop myself from feeling like I’m suddenly different or bigger.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’d say… maybe a few times a month? Then I get this panicky feeling, like it’s just sitting in me and it’s going to turn into weight if I don’t do something. On average… I think it’s usually about once a week. Not every single week in the exact same way, but since my apartment move kind of settled down and I got into a routine again, most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.

It’s usually late at night, after work or after I’ve been trying to be “good” all day, and then I’ll start eating something small and it just turns into… more than I meant. Like way past what I planned, and I feel like I can’t really stop in the moment. Then afterward I get really upset with myself and I start trying to fix it somehow, like making myself throw up, or telling myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a long run even if I’m tired.

So if you’re asking for a number, I’d say maybe once a week on average. Sometimes more if it’s a rough week, but most weeks it’s at least one late-night episode like that.

Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.\n\nOther times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing. It’s more this pressure in my head, like I owe a debt from the night before and I need to pay it back somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks, I’ll end up going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. Usually it’s after I already feel like I went way past the point where I should’ve stopped, and then I start panicking about it just… sitting in me, I guess. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. Then afterward I don’t exactly feel good, it’s more like relief mixed with feeling pretty ashamed that I did it again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every time. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. But I don’t think I’ve had some dramatic weight loss or gain that I could point to and say, “That’s from the eating episodes and then trying to make up for them.”\n\nIt’s more that I’m constantly worried it’s going to change, if that makes sense. Like the fear of gaining weight is there, and that drives a lot of what I do afterward, but as far as actual significant changes… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything major, or at least nothing I’m confident saying is significant.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight what motivates you to try to make up for these episodes of eating by restricting your food intake or purging?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow when I say it out loud, but honestly, yes. It’s the fear of gaining weight, or even just looking like I gained weight.\n\nLike, I work around people all day at the venue, and I’m visible. Guests see me, coworkers see me, and even when people have told me I look too lean or that I don’t need to worry, I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel even a little tighter, or if the scale is up by like a pound, I get this immediate panic feeling. It’s like my brain jumps straight to, “Everyone’s going to notice. But in the moment it feels very real, like I have to do something to undo it before it shows. So yeah, that fear is what pushes me to try to make up for it, whether that’s not eating as much afterward or purging. It’s not really about feeling healthy or anything like that. It’s more like I’m trying to stop that panic and stop myself from feeling like I’m suddenly different or bigger.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’d say… maybe a few times a month? But if I’m estimating, it’s probably around two or three times in a month, maybe a little more in a bad month.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, like I’ll keep picking at things and then suddenly it feels like I crossed some line. Then I get this panicky feeling, like it’s just sitting in me and it’s going to turn into weight if I don’t do something. I do have this feeling afterward like I did something wrong, even though it’s just food and I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll sit there thinking, “Why did I do that again?” and I’ll feel bad about wasting money, or eating things I had meant to save, or just not stopping when I told myself to stop. I’ll start out thinking I’m just having something small or finishing dinner, and then it turns into eating way past what I meant to. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not every time. It’s… it’s more like sometimes, when it feels really bad afterward.

Like if I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or I keep going back for snacks, and then I suddenly realize how much I had, that’s when I start feeling panicky. It feels like it’s just sitting in me, and I get this thought that if I don’t do something right away, it’s going to turn into weight or I’m going to wake up looking different. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels urgent.

So there are times I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. But it’s not after every out-of-control eating episode. Sometimes I just sit with it and feel awful about it, or I try to tell myself I won’t do it again. But yeah, there are definitely times when I do make myself throw up afterward because I can’t stand the feeling of it being in me.

---------- turn 13 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I came in because I’ve been having these times, usually after I work a late shift, where I’ll stop and get food on the way home, like a full takeout meal, and then once I’m back at my apartment I just… keep going. Chips, sweets, leftovers, whatever is around, really.\n\nAnd it’s not like I planned to eat all of it. In the moment it sort of turns into this long stretch of eating, and then afterward I look at the containers or the bags or whatever and I’m kind of shocked and embarrassed by how much I actually ate. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because there’s usually this point where part of me is very aware of what’s happening, like I’m standing there in my kitchen or sitting on the couch and I’m thinking, okay, stop now, put the rest away, you’ve had enough. But then I don’t actually stop.\n\nIt almost feels like I’m watching myself from the outside, if that makes sense. Like I can see my hands reaching for another container or opening another bag, and in my head I’m telling myself not to, but I just keep going anyway. I’ll open the takeout leftovers, then something from the cupboard, then something else, and it keeps going until there’s not really anything left that I had been eating. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time either. It’s more like I can’t get myself to pause long enough to choose differently.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating is over?\n\nPatient: I mean… not great. Mostly embarrassed, I guess. Sometimes disgusted with myself, but even saying that feels kind of dramatic. It’s more like this heavy, sinking feeling, like, “What is wrong with me?” And then I’ll start thinking about how much I ate and replaying it, like the order of everything, what I opened, what I finished, what I should’ve just thrown away or not bought in the first place.\n\nI usually feel really ashamed, especially if there are wrappers or containers out where I can see them. I don’t want anyone to know, even though I live alone, so it’s not like someone is standing there judging me. But I’m judging myself pretty hard. I’ll think I’m an adult, I’m 40, I should be able to just eat dinner like a normal person and be done. And then I get mad at myself for not having that basic control.\n\nIt kind of hangs over me afterward. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but it’s mostly embarrassment and shame, and feeling like I let myself down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, guilt is probably part of it, but I’m not totally sure where guilt ends and shame starts, if that makes sense. I do have this feeling afterward like I did something wrong, even though it’s just food and I know that sounds odd when I say it out loud.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll sit there thinking, “Why did I do that again?” and I’ll feel bad about wasting money, or eating things I had meant to save, or just not stopping when I told myself to stop. So yes, there’s guilt in that way. But the bigger feeling is usually embarrassment and being really disappointed in myself. Like I’ve broken some promise to myself that I didn’t even say out loud.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Lately it’s been… most weeks, I’d say. Since my apartment move kind of settled down and my routine became more predictable, it’s usually at least once a week that I’ll have one of those late-night episodes.\n\nIt tends to happen at night, after work or after I’ve been home for a while, when I’m by myself. I’ll start out thinking I’m just having something small or finishing dinner, and then it turns into eating way past what I meant to. Not every single night or anything like that, but most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.\n\nAnd then afterward I usually try to make up for it somehow, like throwing up, or telling myself I’m just going to skip breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a really long run even if I don’t actually feel up to it. So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to make up for these episodes, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. It feels more like a punishment, I guess, or like I’m trying to undo what happened the night before.\n\nOther times I’ll skip breakfast before my shift. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing. It’s more this pressure in my head, like I owe a debt from the night before and I need to pay it back somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not every single time, but sometimes after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks, I’ll end up going into the bathroom and making myself throw up. Usually it’s after I already feel like I went way past the point where I should’ve stopped, and then I start panicking about it just… sitting in me, I guess. Like if I don’t do something, it’s going to turn into weight.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, and I’m embarrassed saying it out loud. In the moment it feels like this urgent thing, like I have to get it out of me so I can stop thinking about it. Then afterward I don’t exactly feel good, it’s more like relief mixed with feeling pretty ashamed that I did it again.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every time. I want to be clear about that, because sometimes I’ll just sit with the awful feeling afterward, or I’ll tell myself I won’t eat the next morning, but there are times where I do make myself throw up.\n\nI’d say it happens sometimes after those bigger episodes, especially when I’ve gotten home and eaten too much takeout or a bunch of snacks and then I start feeling panicky, like it’s just sitting in me. That’s usually when I’ll go into the bathroom and do it. It feels like, if I don’t get it out, it’s going to turn into weight, even though I know that probably sounds… not logical.\n\nSo, frequency-wise, I don’t know the exact number. It’s not daily, and it’s not after every episode, but it does happen sometimes when I feel like I’ve really gone overboard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes and your attempts to make up for them?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I mean, I notice my weight, probably more than I should, but I don’t know that I can say there’s been some huge, clear change over time because of it.\n\nIt kind of feels like it goes up and down a little, and I’ll get really focused on those small changes, like if my clothes feel tighter one day or the number is different, I’ll read way too much into it. But I don’t think I’ve had some dramatic weight loss or gain that I could point to and say, “That’s from the eating episodes and then trying to make up for them.”\n\nIt’s more that I’m constantly worried it’s going to change, if that makes sense. Like the fear of gaining weight is there, and that drives a lot of what I do afterward, but as far as actual significant changes… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything major, or at least nothing I’m confident saying is significant.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight what motivates you to try to make up for these episodes of eating by restricting your food intake or purging?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow when I say it out loud, but honestly, yes. It’s the fear of gaining weight, or even just looking like I gained weight.\n\nLike, I work around people all day at the venue, and I’m visible. Guests see me, coworkers see me, and even when people have told me I look too lean or that I don’t need to worry, I still don’t really believe it. If my pants feel even a little tighter, or if the scale is up by like a pound, I get this immediate panic feeling. It’s like my brain jumps straight to, “Everyone’s going to notice. They’re going to think I let myself go.”\n\nAnd I know one pound probably isn’t something anyone else can see. I can say that logically. But in the moment it feels very real, like I have to do something to undo it before it shows. So yeah, that fear is what pushes me to try to make up for it, whether that’s not eating as much afterward or purging. It’s not really about feeling healthy or anything like that. It’s more like I’m trying to stop that panic and stop myself from feeling like I’m suddenly different or bigger.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say you make yourself throw up after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’d say… maybe a few times a month? It’s hard to give an exact number because I don’t really track it, and honestly I try not to think of it that way. But if I’m estimating, it’s probably around two or three times in a month, maybe a little more in a bad month.\n\nIt’s usually after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or snacks, like I’ll keep picking at things and then suddenly it feels like I crossed some line. Then I get this panicky feeling, like it’s just sitting in me and it’s going to turn into weight if I don’t do something. That’s when I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.\n\nSo… not every week like clockwork, and not after every time I overeat, but sometimes. A few times a month is probably the closest answer.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week would you say you have one of these episodes of eating where you feel out of control?\n\nPatient: On average… I think it’s usually about once a week. Not every single week in the exact same way, but since my apartment move kind of settled down and I got into a routine again, most weeks there’s at least one night where it happens.\n\nIt’s usually late at night, after work or after I’ve been trying to be “good” all day, and then I’ll start eating something small and it just turns into… more than I meant. Like way past what I planned, and I feel like I can’t really stop in the moment. Then afterward I get really upset with myself and I start trying to fix it somehow, like making myself throw up, or telling myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast the next day, or forcing myself to go on a long run even if I’m tired.\n\nSo if you’re asking for a number, I’d say maybe once a week on average. Sometimes more if it’s a rough week, but most weeks it’s at least one late-night episode like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after every episode of eating where you feel out of control?\n\nPatient: No, not every time. It’s… it’s more like sometimes, when it feels really bad afterward.\n\nLike if I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or I keep going back for snacks, and then I suddenly realize how much I had, that’s when I start feeling panicky. It feels like it’s just sitting in me, and I get this thought that if I don’t do something right away, it’s going to turn into weight or I’m going to wake up looking different. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels urgent.\n\nSo there are times I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up. But it’s not after every out-of-control eating episode. Sometimes I just sit with it and feel awful about it, or I try to tell myself I won’t do it again. But yeah, there are definitely times when I do make myself throw up afterward because I can’t stand the feeling of it being in me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F50.2", "F50.5"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s less often than that. I don’t think it’s most of the time, no.

It’s more like… sometimes, after I get home and I’ve eaten too much takeout or I’ve been snacking and it turns into more than I meant, I get this really uncomfortable feeling that it’s just sitting in me. And then I start thinking about it turning into weight, like if I don’t do something right away it’s going to stay there or show up on me somehow. That’s usually when I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself throw up.

But it’s not every time I feel out of control eating, and I wouldn’t say it’s most episodes. It’s just… when the panic is bad enough, I guess.

So… yeah, I guess if I’m being honest, about weekly, sometimes more, but usually at least once in a week.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to make up for these episodes, can you tell me more about what you mean by that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… so, when I say “make up for it,” I mean I’ll try to sort of cancel it out the next day. Like if I had a bad night where I ate a lot and felt out of control, I wake up already thinking I need to do something about it.\n\nA lot of times that means I’ll go run extra miles on the trail in the morning, even if I’m tired or my legs feel heavy or I didn’t sleep well. I’ll tell myself, “Just go farther today,” or “You have to burn some of that off.” It doesn’t really feel like a normal workout then. I’ll be getting ready for work and even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, “No, you don’t need breakfast after what you ate last night.” And then I’ll just have coffee or water and go in. I know that probably sounds stupid, because it doesn’t actually erase anything, but in the moment it feels like I have to do something or I’ll just sit with that gross, guilty feeling all day.\n\nIt’s not always some big planned thing.
